## Page 1


COMPLETE ISOCATEGORICAL CLASSIFICATION OF
GROUPS OF ORDER 64 VIA GAP
SHOKI SATO
Abstract. The classification of finite groups under monoidal equivalence is
a fundamental topic in the study of finite quantum groups. While a complete
classification has been established for all groups of order strictly less than
64, the case for order 64 has remained limited to the construction of specific
examples. In this study, we achieve the complete classification for groups of
order 64 by developing an original computational approach using GAP. We
describe our methodology and demonstrate that there exist exactly two pairs
of non-isomorphic isocategorical groups of this order.
1. Introduction
The classification of finite groups under monoidal equivalence is a fundamental
problem in the study of finite quantum groups and fusion categories. Two groups
G and H are said to be monoidally equivalent (or isocategorical) if their respective
representation categories Rep(G) and Rep(H) are equivalent as tensor categories.
To date, a complete classification based on this equivalence has been established
for all groups of order strictly less than 64 [4]. However, the case of order 64 = 26
has remained a significant open challenge. The difficulty of this specific order
arises from the structural requirements for a group G to possess a non-trivial
monoidally equivalent partner. It is a well-established result that if Rep(G) ≃
Rep(H) as tensor categories, then H must be isomorphic to a ”twisted” group
Gω constructed via a G-invariant non-degenerate 2-cocycle ω ∈Z2( b
N, T) on a
normal abelian subgroup N ⊴G [2, 8].
Furthermore, order 64 is recognized as the minimal order for which non-
isomorphic, yet monoidally equivalent, pairs of groups exist [2].
The most
prominent examples are the Izumi–Kosaki pairs, which were originally identi-
fied through the study of subfactors [5]. Despite the theoretical importance of
these examples, the sheer number of groups of order 64, totaling 267 distinct iso-
morphism classes, and the complexity of their internal subgroup structures have
prevented an exhaustive classification until now.
In this paper, we resolve this problem by combining elementary algebraic argu-
ments with a systematic computational approach. We have developed an original
2020 Mathematics Subject Classification. Primary 16T20; Secondary 46L67.
Key words and phrases. Finite groups, Compact quantum groups.

By evaluating G-invariance and non-

the complete classification of groups of order 64 under monoidal equivalence.
2. Preliminaries
2.1. Finite quantum group. Let A be a finite dimensional C∗-algebra and
∆: A →A⊗A a unital faithful ∗-homomorphism. A pair (A, ∆) is called a finite
quantum group if ∆satisfies the cancellation properties ∆(A)(1A ⊗A) = A ⊗A
and ∆(A)(A ⊗1A) = A ⊗A. It is denoted by G := (A, ∆) and A := C(G)
as is standard.
Let ι: C(G) →C(G) be the identity map.
The two maps
S : C(G) →C(G), ε: C(G) →C are respectively called the antipode, and the
counit if they satisfy the equalities
(ε ⊗ι)∆(a) = (ι ⊗ε)∆(a) = a
m(S ⊗ι)∆(a) = m(ι ⊗S)∆(a) = ε(a)1
for all a ∈C(G), where m: C(G) ⊗C(G) →C(G) is the multiplication map.
It is well known that any finite quantum group possesses a unique antipode and
counit.
Example 2.1. Let G be a finite group and C(G) denote the unital C∗-algebra
of C-valued functions on G equipped with pointwise operations. We define the
comultiplication ∆: C(G) →C(G) ⊗C(G) ≃C(G × G) by ∆(f)(g, h) := f(gh)
for f ∈C(G) and g, h ∈G. It is straightforward to verify that (C(G), ∆) is a
finite quantum group.
Example 2.2. Let G be a finite group and C[G] the group algebra of G. We
define the comultiplication ∆: C[G] →C[G] ⊗C[G] by ∆(g) := g ⊗g for g ∈
G. Then (C[G], ∆) is a finite quantum group. When G is abelian, the Foulier
transform induces an isomorphism C[G] ≃C( bG) as unital C∗-algebras, where bG
denotes the Pontryagin dual of G.
A finite quantum group (A, ∆) is called cocommutative if ∆satisfies the equa-
tion ∆= ∆op, where ∆op := Σ∆and Σ: A ⊗A →A ⊗A is the flip map. It is
known that (A, ∆) is cocommutative if and only if it is isomorphic, as a finite
quantum group, to the group algebra (C[G], ∆) for some finite group G. As seen
in Example 2.2, if G is an abelian group, then the function algebra (C(G), ∆) is
cocommutative.
Let G be a finite quantum group and bA := C(G)∗. For any φ, ψ ∈bA, we define
the product φ·ψ, the involution φ∗, and the dual comultiplication b∆: bA →bA⊗bA
by
(φ · ψ)(a) := (φ ⊗ψ)(∆(a)),
φ∗(a) := φ(S(a)),
b∆(φ)(a ⊗b) := φ(ab).

Definition 2.3 (Etingof and Gelaki [2]). Two finite quantum groups G1 and
G2 are said to be isocategorical (or monoidal equivalent) if their representation
categories Rep(G1) and Rep(G2) are equivalent as C∗-tensor categories in the
sense of [6, Section 2].
2.3. 2-cocycles. Let G be a finite group. A unitary element ω ∈C[G] ⊗C[G] is
called a 2-cocycle if it satisfies the following cocycle relation:
(∆⊗ι)(ω)(ω ⊗1) = (ι ⊗∆)(ω)(1 ⊗ω).
(2.5)
We denote the set of all such 2-cocycles by Z2( bG, T). Two 2-cocycles ω1, ω2 ∈
Z2( bG, T) are said to be equivalent, denoted by ω1 ∼ω2, if there exists a unitary
ξ ∈C[G] such that
ω2 = ∆(ξ∗)ω1(ξ ⊗ξ).
(2.6)
The set of equivalence classes of 2-cocycles for G is denoted by H2( bG, T).
Example 2.4. When G is an abelian group, C[G] is isomorphic to C( bG), where
the function algebra on the Pontryagin dual bG, introduced as before.
Under
this identification, we can regard a 2-cocycle ω ∈Z2( bG, T) as an element of
C( bG) ⊗C( bG) ∼= C( bG × bG). The cocycle relation (2.5) is then equivalent to the
following equation:
ω(ρ, σ)ω(ρσ, τ) = ω(σ, τ)ω(ρ, στ)
for all ρ, σ, τ ∈bG,
which is the standard T-valued 2-cocycle condition on bG. Furthermore, two 2-
cocycles ω1, ω2 ∈C( bG) ⊗C( bG) are equivalent in the sense of (2.6) if and only if
there exists a function ξ : bG →T such that
ω1(σ, τ)ω2(σ, τ) = ξ(σ)ξ(τ)ξ(στ)
for all σ, τ ∈bG.
This recovers the standard equivalence relation for 2-cocycles in H2( bG, T). We
denote the coboundary by ∂ξ(σ, τ) := ξ(σ)ξ(τ)ξ(στ). For a comprehensive treat-
ment of group cohomology, we refer the reader to [1].

(3) Let α: N1 →Aut(Mn(C)) and β : N2 →Aut(Mn(C)) be the commuting
actions.
Since Mn(C) is a finite dimensional factor, there exist unitaries
un ∈Mn(C) such that αn = Ad un for each n ∈N1. For any m ∈N2 and
n ∈N1, the commutativity of the actions implies:
Ad(βm(un)) = βm ◦Ad un ◦β−1
m = αn = Ad un.
Since Ad(a) = Ad(b) on Mn(C) implies a = λb for some λ ∈C×, there exists
a scalar µn(m) ∈T such that βm(un) = µn(m)un. One can easily verify that
the map πn : N2 →T defined by m 7→µn(m) is a group homomorphism,
representing a one dimensional representation of N2. By the ergodicity of the
action α, the commutant of {un | n ∈N1} is trivial, which implies:
Mn(C) = span{un | n ∈N1}.
(2.7)
Equation (2.7) shows that Mn(C), as an N2-module under the action β, de-
composes into a direct sum of one-dimensional invariant subspaces Cun. Ac-
cording to the full multiplicity property of the action β, every irreducible
representation of N2 must appear as a subrepresentation of Mn(C). Since we
have shown that all irreducible subrepresentations in the decomposition of
Mn(C) are one-dimensional, it follows that all irreducible representations of
N2 are one dimensional. This is equivalent to saying that N2 is an abelian
group. By symmetry, N1 is also abelian.
(4) Suppose, toward a contradiction, that N1 is a cyclic group.
It is a well-
known result in group cohomology that the second cohomology group of a
finite cyclic group with coefficients in T is trivial, i.e., H2(N1, T) ∼= 0. As
established in the proof of (3), the action of N1 on Mn(C) is determined by a
projective unitary representation u associated with a 2-cocycle ω ∈Z2(N1, T).
Since H2(N1, T) = 0, the 2-cocycle ω is a coboundary, meaning there exists
a map ξ : N1 →T such that ω(g, h) = ξ(g)ξ(h)ξ(gh)−1. Consequently, the
projective representation u is equivalent to an ordinary linear representation
π: N1 →U(Cn) given by π(g) = ξ(g)−1ug.Since N1 is abelian (by part 3), all
of its irreducible linear representations must be one-dimensional. However,
we have shown in the proof of (3) that u (and thus π) is an irreducible
representation of dimension n. This forces n = 1. According to the result
from part (1), the order of the group is |N1| = n2 = 12 = 1. This directly
contradicts our assumption that |N1| ≥2. Therefore, N1 cannot be a cyclic
group.
□
2.6. Isocategorical group and C∗-algebra. Let G be a finite group and ω ∈
Z2( bG, T) be a 2-cocycle. We can construct a finite quantum group Gω, which is
isocategorical to G, in the following manner. Using the isomorphisms in (2.4),
we define a new coproduct b∆ω : C( bG) →C( bG) ⊗C( bG) by
b∆ω(φ) := ω∗b∆(φ)ω
for φ ∈C( bG). The pair (C( bG), b∆ω) then forms the dual of a new finite quantum
group. We define Gω to be the dual finite quantum group of (C( bG), b∆ω).

## Page 8


The following result is a cornerstone in the classification of finite quantum
groups with equivalent representation categories. It was established through the
works of Etingof–Gelaki [2].
Theorem 2.7 (Etingof and Gelaki [2]). Let G be a finite group and ω ∈Z2( bG, T)
be a 2-cocycle. Then the representation category of Gω is equivalent to that of G as
a C∗-tensor category. Conversely, any finite quantum group whose representation
category is equivalent to Rep(G) as a C∗-tensor category is obtained via such a
2-cocycle twist.
Remark 2.8. It should be noted that Gω is not necessarily cocommutative. That
is to say, Gω is not necessarily isomorphic to a classical finite group, despite the
fact that it is isocategorical to G.
Furthermore, Wassermann established a correspondence between 2-cocycles
and ergodic actions on C∗-algebras.
Theorem 2.9 (Wassermann [8]). There is a natural bijection between full mul-
tiplicity ergodic actions and H2( bG, T).
Summarizing these results, for the study of a finite group G1, the following are
essentially equivalent:
• a finite quantum group G2 which is isocategorical to G1;
• a 2-cocycle ω ∈Z2(c
G1, T);
• an ergodic action α: G1 →Aut(A) of full multiplicity on a finite-
dimensional C∗-algebra A.
Izumi and Kosaki characterized the conditions under which the quantum group
Gω, twisted by a 2-cocycle, is cocommutative. We only provide an outline of the
proof.
Theorem 2.10 (Izumi and Kosaki [5]). Let G be a finite group and ω ∈Z2( bG, T)
2-cocycle. Then the following two conditions are equivalent.
(1) Gω is cocommutative.
(2) There exist an abelian normal subgroup N ⊂G and non-degenerate 2-
cocycle ω1 ∈Z2( b
N, T) satisfying ω ∼ω1 in Z2( bG, T) and ω1 is G-
invariant in Z2( b
N, T).
We assume that N and ω = ω1 satisfy the condition (2) in Theorem 2.10. Since
N is abelian, by the duality we can regard ω as a 2-cocycle in Z2( b
N, T).
Theorem 2.11 (Izumi and Kosaki [5]). With these notations we have
(1) There exist ξg : b
N →T for each g ∈G and η: G × G →N such that for
any pair σ, τ ∈b
N we have
ω(σg, τ g) = ∂ξg(σ, τ)ω(σ, τ)
ξg1(σ)ξg2(σg1) = σ(η(g1, g2))ξg1g2(σ).

Therefore, the problem of finding a group isocategorical to G reduces to the
study of 2-cocycles ω on certain normal abelian subgroups N. The following
results by Shimizu, Izumi, Kosaki, Goyvaerts, and Meir are fundamental to our
research.
Theorem 2.13 (Shimizu [7]). If two finite groups G1, G2 are isocategorical, then
for each positive integer n, the number of elements of order n in G1 is equal to
the number of elements of order n in G2.
Definition 2.14. Let G be a finite group and D(n) denote the number of elements
in the set {g ∈G | ord(g) = n}. Let d1, d2, . . . , dm be the divisors of the order of
G such that d1 < d2 < · · · < dm. The order list of G, denoted by List(G), is the
ordered set:
List(G) := {D(d1), D(d2), . . . , D(dm)}.
The order list is an invariant of isocategorical groups.
For groups of order less than 64, a complete classification regarding isocategori-
cal equivalence has been established. A finite group G is said to be isocategorically
rigid if any group isocategorical to G is necessarily isomorphic to G.
Theorem 2.15 (Goyvaerts and Meir [4, Proposition 6.2]). All finite groups of
order less than 64 are isocategorically rigid.
However, there exist groups of order 64 that are not isocategorically rigid. We
will discuss the detailed construction of such cases in Section 4.
Theorem 2.16 (Izumi and Kosaki [5, Theorem 4.6]). For a certain group G of
order 64, there exists a 2-cocycle ω ∈Z2( bG, T) such that G and Gω are non-
isomorphic.
3. The case of order 64 groups
Throughout this section, let G be a group of order 64. We denote the cyclic
group of order k ∈N by Ck. For a direct product group C = Ck1 × · · · × Ckn, an
element c ∈C is represented as a vector (c1, . . . , cn) or its transpose (c1, . . . , cn)t,
where each ci is an integer satisfying 0 ≤ci < ki. Similarly, an element of the
Pontryagin dual bC is denoted by σ =
\
(σ1, . . . , σn) or σ =
\
(σ1, . . . , σn)t.

Theorem 3.1. Let N be a non-trivial abelian normal subgroup of G whose order
is a square. Suppose that N admits a non-degenerate 2-cocycle ω ∈Z2( b
N, T).
Then N is isomorphic to one of the following groups:
C2 × C2, C4 × C4, C2 × C2 × C2 × C2.
Proof. Since |G| = 64 and |N| is a square, the possible orders for a proper non-
trivial subgroup N are |N| = 4 or 16. Given that N is abelian and non-cyclic
(by Lemma 2.6), the candidates for N are:
C2 × C2,
C4 × C4,
C2 × C2 × C2 × C2,
and
C4 × C2 × C2.
We shall show that C4 × C2 × C2 is excluded due to the requirement of non-
degeneracy. Let βω : b

4. GAP Implementation for Isocategorical Equivalence
The computational results presented in this paper rely on algorithms imple-
mented in GAP. For a detailed description of the scripts and functions, the reader
is referred to the GitHub repository isocategorical-group-structure. The
full source code used to compute isocategorical groups in this study is available for
download in the Sourcecode.g file at the following URL: https://github.com/
barley-tea-3416-cloud/isocategorical-group-structure/releases/tag/1.
0.1.
4.1. Overview of GAP. GAP (Groups, Algorithms, and Programming) [3] is
a software system for computational discrete algebra, with a particular emphasis
on computational finite group theory.
GAP provides a robust framework for
investigating the structural properties of groups; in addition to standard tasks
such as verifying the normality of subgroups, it facilitates complex computations
involving group isomorphisms, automorphisms, and finitely presented groups.
14


## Page 15

GAP, we must address the following two computational problems:

Lemma 4.3. Let N be a normal subgroup of G isomorphic to C×2

(1) The G-invariance of ω1 is equivalent to the G-invariance of ω3.

The implementation details for computing the factor η(g, h) are provided in
GitHub repository5. The GAP function Eta C2xC2(N, g, h) calculates the value
of η(g, h) ∈N for a given pair of group elements g, h ∈G through the following
procedure:
(1) Extraction of Matrices: The function first obtains the action matrices
A(g), A(h), and A(gh) by calling Checkginv C2xC2.
(2) Component Retrieval: It extracts the individual entries (k, l, m, n for
g; a, b, c, d for h; and p, q, r, s for gh) from these matrices to serve as
variables for the formula.
(3) Computation:
Using the components, it evaluates the expressions
check1:= η(g, h)1 and check2:= η(g, h)2 as defined in Lemma 4.4.
(4) Normalization and Output: The results are divided by 2 and taken
modulo 2. Finally, the function returns the result as a group element of
N in the form gen1^want1 * gen2^want2, where gen1 and gen2 are the
generators of N.
We now describe the implementation of the product relations for Gω1. First, we
index the elements of G as {g1, g2, . . . , g64} using the GAP function Elements(G).
Let Free64 be the free group generated by 64 generators {f1, f2, . . . , f64}. For
any pair of elements gi, gj ∈G, there exists a unique index k ∈{1, . . . , 64} such

The product in Gω1, defined by ˜gi · ˜gj = ˜gk, is then
represented in Free64 by the relation:
fifj = fk.
By computing these relations for all pairs (i, j) and taking the quotient of Free64
by the resulting set of relators, we construct a finitely presented group isomorphic

Since we can now calculate the structures of the subgroups and the group Gω
for a given ω, Problem 4.2 is now settled.
4.3.4. Comprehensive Isomorphism Search Across All Candidate Subgroups. To
systematically identify isocategorical pairs, we implement a unified procedure
that evaluates all suitable normal subgroups N within a given group G, regardless
of their specific isomorphism type. This automated pipeline is primarily handled
by the GAP function GwStructure(G). As detailed in GitHub repository 9, the
function operates through the following steps:
(1) Abelian Check: It first verifies whether G is abelian, as the twisted
construction Gω for abelian groups is well-understood.
(2) Subgroup Retrieval: For non-abelian groups, it utilizes ObtainedSubgroups(G)
to identify all normal subgroups N isomorphic to C×2
2 , C×2
4 , or C×4
2 .
(3) Automated Evaluation: For each identified subgroup N, the func-
tion automatically dispatches the appropriate subroutine,
such as
GwStructureC2xC2(G, N) or GwStructureC4xC4(G, N), to test for G-
invariance and construct the twisted group Gω.
(4) Isomorphism and Classification: Finally, it determines whether Gω
is isomorphic to G. If they are non-isomorphic, the function identifies
the SmallGroup ID of Gω by comparing it with other groups in the same
order category.
Remark 4.6. At the time GwStructure(G) was developed, a standard method
for implementing twisted groups derived from C×4
2
had not yet been established.

mentation and verification independently.
By applying this function to a collection of groups, we can exhaustively search
for isocategorical but non-isomorphic pairs across a specific order.
5. Isocategorical Classification Tables for Groups of Order 64
5.1. Notes on GAP. The structural descriptions of finite groups in this paper
follow the conventions used by the GAP computer algebra system, which are
largely based on the ATLAS of Finite Groups.
The symbols used to denote
group extensions are summarized as follows:
• A × B denotes the direct product of A and B.
• A : B denotes a split extension of A by B, which is isomorphic to a semi-direct
product A ⋊B. In this case, G contains a subgroup isomorphic to B that
complements A.
• A.B denotes a non-split extension of A by B. This represents a group G that
fits into a short exact sequence 1 →A →G →B →1, but unlike the split
case, G does not contain a subgroup isomorphic to B that complements A.
Furthermore, in GAP, all groups of order 64 are generated by at most six
elements; therefore, we identify each group in this paper using the multiplication
table of its generators. As noted in Section 1, since the list of element orders
is invariant between isocategorical groups, we organize the following descriptions
into subsections grouped by these order lists.
5.2. Example of Table. We consider the group GIK as an example. The first
table displays the product relations between the generators {fi}6
i=1 of GIK.

6. conclusion
Based on the computational results presented in the previous sections, we es-
tablish the following theorem regarding the classification of isocategorical groups
of order 64.
Theorem 6.1. There exist exactly two pairs of non-isomorphic groups of order
64 that are monoidally equivalent.
168


## Page 169


The two pairs stated in Theorem 6.1 are described in Theorem 6.2 and Theorem
6.3, respectively.
Theorem 6.2. Let G1 and G2 be the groups generated by the elements defined in
the following group tables. While G1 and G2 are not isomorphic as groups, they
are monoidally equivalent. The order list for both groups is given by the list:
List(G1) = List(G2) = {1, 19, 44, 0, 0, 0, 0}.
Furthermore, G1 is isomorphic to the Izumi-Kosaki group GIK.

Theorem 6.3. Let G3 and G4 be the groups generated by the elements defined in
the following group tables. While G3 and G4 are not isomorphic as groups, they
are monoidally equivalent. The order list for both groups is given by the list:
List(G3) = List(G4) = {1, 31, 32, 0, 0, 0, 0}.
